i-1024368cfae3c7fa-5	Mr President, I would like to Today we are debating the report by the Committee on Foreign Affairs, UU9 on the strengthening of the export control of military equipment. For many countries, arms exports are a foreign policy tool to gain benefits at the expense of other countries, not least with regard to the world's various powers. But for Sweden this is not the case, but here all decisions on arms exports are weighed against the consequences for other countries and their inhabitants. In this way, our export control of armaments has developed into what it is today, namely the second most stringent legislation in the world. Only Japan has stricter legislation. It may be good to recall this, Mr President, because sometimes in the debate it sounds like our arms exports are completely unregulated. Such a claim is therefore, to say the least, wrong. In its proposal, the government proposes the introduction of a democracy criterion. This has been a recurring topic of debate in these contexts in this House, and we have in the past expressed cautiously in favour of its introduction. As always in foreign policy, it is important to understand the fact that not all countries work just like Sweden. That is why we have stated in the past that a democracy criterion should not be what we call absolute, but rather a more nuanced assessment. We also think that the bill has landed in this. Mr President, I would like to Sweden, Swedish defence capabilities and our freedom of alliance have benefited from the fact that we have had a well-functioning domestic defence industry. Thanks to its availability, we, as a country, have been able to remain neutral and independent of the willingness of other states to sell war material to us – something which was not least positive for the detour when the Cold War was as cold as it was and which is also positive now that the security situation around the Baltic Sea has deteriorated. However, in order to survive, the Swedish defence industry depends entirely on its exports. A good example of this is the fighter aircraft JAS 39 Gripen. Thanks to the sale of Gripen to Brazil, Saab and Sweden have been given the opportunity to continue developing the aircraft and maintain our domestic aviation industry. If the sale had not been carried out, this would probably not have been possible – something that would have led to very negative consequences for our aviation industry, the ability of the Swedish Air Force and our taxpayers. One condition that is also absolutely crucial for our exports of armaments is the consequential supply. The country that buys an armaments system from us must be able to rely on them to buy ammunition, spare parts and upgrades for a long time – decades – from us. Therefore, there is today a presumption of consequential supply, which can only be broken by changing conditions for the unconditional obstacles – internationally binding agreements, binding sanctions and international law rules on neutral states during war. Now a slight slippage is proposed in the assessment that is difficult to interpret. However, it is easy to note that subsequent deliveries do not require stricter assessment. Other armed forces buying supplies from us have the same security of supply needs as our own armed forces. If we want to sell equipment systems, we must also sell ammunition for, spare parts for and upgrades to what we have exported. To delete from the list of what should be considered as consequent deliveries will give Swedish industry a bad reputation. With this comes the risk of reduced exports and in the longer term dismantling of the Swedish defence industry, with negative consequences for Sweden as a result. Mr President, I would like to What we Swedish democrats may respond to most strongly in the government bill is the proposed new PGU criterion. The criterion requires Sweden to assess what is best for a recipient country. Let us ask ourselves whether a country really can afford war material or whether it should instead purchase food, schoolbooks or something else that is considered more important. We in Sweden will therefore assess what is best for an individual country, which can be very difficult. One question one might ask is what our Finnish brothers would have said if, instead of ammunition, they had only received bread from us during the winter war. We believe that it must be the recipient country that assesses what it needs and what it can afford. If the country's regime is aggressive or otherwise unstable, it still falls under the democratic and human rights criteria. We therefore totally reject the introduction of a PGU criterion. Such a criterion makes Swedish export control completely different from the other countries in sex nation cooperation, and instead of making the rules more similar to each other, it is exactly the opposite. With four different conditional obstacles in the export control guidelines, we have the tools we need: the MR criterion, the democracy criterion, the conflict criterion and the diversion criterion. The PGU criterion is unclear and dangerous and should therefore not be included. Mr President, I would like to It is completely naive to believe that exports in the world will stop if Sweden puts a stop to itself and stops exporting. Of course, it is then someone else who sells – someone who does not live up to the already high standards Sweden has. It would be better if Sweden worked to ensure that other countries caught up with Sweden and the already high standards we have, instead of continuing to make ourselves more difficult and impossible to deal with. We cannot sit and judge from the PGU whether countries should buy defence equipment from Sweden or not. It is a matter for individual countries to assess their own security policy situation. We therefore believe that the PGU criterion will limit Swedish arms exports in an unpredictable way and that our international partners risk breaking contacts with Sweden. Other countries' armaments export producers have already begun to work for competitive purposes to crowd Swedish companies out of important materiel cooperation. Only the discussion on arms exports and the KEX investigation, which led up to the government proposal, have adversely affected the Swedish defence industry. Presuming buyer countries have shown uncertainty as to whether Swedish exports can be trusted in the future. The defence industry can testify to this. So the situation is critical. We in politics should be grateful that the Swedish defence industry remains. The conditions are already difficult. If exports are severely reduced, Swedish defence companies will have to either leave the country or, with their capital, leave the industry. Both options would be a disaster for Swedish armaments supply and our freedom of alliance. If Sweden is forced to trade all its armaments abroad in the future, it will lead to increased costs, with the result that we get less armaments for the grant – something that will affect our military capabilities very negatively. Our defence industry is also very technology driven and invests more in research than Chalmers and KTH do together. It is also something that would disappear if defence companies were forced to move abroad or lay down their activities. One should also remember all the jobs in the country created by the defence industry both directly and indirectly. Another reason to protect our defence industry is that access to our own advanced technology opens up the foreign market for Sweden. Only those with their own advanced technology can negotiate for themselves the most modern armaments from other countries. Mr President, I would like to In view of this, we are calling for the proposal to be rejected and for reservation to be accepted 1.
i-1024368cfae3c7fa-22	Mr President, I would like to Swedish arms exports have long been examples of double standards. While the government talks about the importance of democracy, peace and women's rights, Sweden armes belligerent dictatorships that systematically violate human rights. It became clear both during the Arab Spring and now, when six out of ten countries participating in the brutal warfare of the Saudi-led coalition in Yemen have purchased Swedish weapons. In countries such as India, South Africa and Brazil, our country Sweden is too many closely linked to corruption-scanned arms trades. The fact that Sweden sold arms to belligerent dictatorships during the Arab Spring aroused anger and outrage and led to the Riksdag deciding in May 2011 that the government would return with a new legislation that tightens the control of arms exports to dictatorships. It took the government over a year to set up a committee to review arms exports. In June 2015, the Committee submitted its final report. Just over two years later, the government submitted a proposal which broadly follows the proposals presented by the KEX investigation. The new regulatory framework is proposed to enter into force on 15 April 2018, almost seven years after the Parliament's announcement. It has been shocking, to say the least, to see how the government has been able in just over six months to implement legislative changes that severely restrict the possibility for people in Sweden to seek refuge from war and oppression, while at the same time having taken nearly seven years to make proposals to limit the sale of the weapons that create this repression and to force people to flee. During these seven years, Sweden has exported war material to non-democrats for about SEK 20 billion. The responsibility for this rests with both the present and the former governments. The Left Party was the driving force behind the decision to tighten the regulatory framework for arms exports, and we welcome the fact that a proposal is now being made. This means some positive changes in clarifying the government's responsibility for arms exports. It also limits the definition of consequential supplies and increases the transparency of exports. On the main issue, however, we believe that the proposal is inadequate and will probably make very little difference. Mr President, I would like to In our motion to the Government's proposal, we have made several suggestions on how the regulatory framework can be changed in a more restrictive direction, but also on transparency and on the import of war material. We in the Left want to stop the export of armaments to dictatorships and human rights abuses. We have therefore put forward a proposal to this effect to enable Parliament to comply with the decision it took in May 2011. I hereby request the approval of reservation 2. The bill presented states that the democratic status must be a central condition for determining whether or not the state in question is appropriate to sell arms to and that serious shortcomings in the democratic status must constitute obstacles to exports. This sounds good, but there is still to be an overall assessment. We have seen too many times how the interests of the arms industry have gone before any democratic shortcomings. That is why the Left Party wants to see a real stop to arms exports to dictatorships. That is why we believe that the democracy criterion should be formulated as a requirement. Also in terms of respect for human rights in the recipient countries, the wording is too weak. The ISP – the authority that assesses whether arms exports should be granted – itself says that the proposal does not strengthen the current regulatory framework, which allows arms exports to several serious human rights abuses. Mr President, I would like to The government often points out that it is the first in the world to pursue a feminist foreign policy. We in the Left Party have on several occasions praised the government's efforts to strengthen women's rights in the world. However, in the Swedish policy for the export of armaments, the feminist perspective is lacking – as is the government's new proposal. In the list of what according to practice should be perceived as serious crimes, for some strange reason, does not mention violations of women's rights. The final report of the KEX study highlights the legitimising effect of arms exports and why this should play a role in decisions on exports to dictatorships or human rights abuses. Writings on the legitimizing effect of armaments exports can be found in the bill by reference to KEX's work but not as part of the government's own assessment. We in the Left Party believe that such wording is needed and would be an important guide in deciding on permits. Under the current regulatory framework, it is relatively easy to obtain a licence for the export of armaments in the case of subsequent deliveries. We can see from the example of Pakistan that the regulatory framework for consequential supplies has been permitting. Although no new business with Pakistan has been granted since 2007 due to the serious situation in the country, we see how Swedish exports of armaments continue in the form of consequential deliveries in 2017. The definition of consequential deliveries will be narrowed down by the bill, which is a welcome step. However, we want to see all exports tested according to the regulatory framework and subject to the criterion of human rights and democracy. The Government's proposal implies some increased transparency and transparency. In addition to full members of the Export Control Council, alternates shall also be appointed to enable discussions on the confidential matters between party colleagues. In addition, the Government's report to the Riksdag will be developed, and the Government will submit statements regarding export control issues to the Swedish Advisory Council for Foreign Affairs. The Left Party welcomes these changes, but considers that further proposals to increase transparency and transparency are needed, for example as regards who appoints representatives to the EKR and that their positions are made public except when this clearly conflicts with confidentiality. In 2016, Sweden has exported weapons to Saudi Arabia, Qatar, Pakistan, Oman, United Arab Emirates, Singapore, Malaysia, Mexico, Thailand and Jordan. All these states are classified as dictatorships or as states with major democratic shortcomings, according to Freedom House. These lists usually look like that year after year, and this can continue to be the case with the legislative changes that the government is now proposing. In order to determine whether the intention behind the proposal – and the directives for the KEX investigation – has been met, the changes in the regulatory framework should be evaluated after three years, so that we can clearly see whether they have involved the enhanced export control of war material intended. Mr President, I would like to The Left Party wants Sweden to be a country that consistently stands up for democracy and a country that actively works for peace and disarmament and condemns any violation of human rights, regardless of where, by whom or with what motives they are committed. For Sweden to be able to do this with credibility, we must stop arming dictatorships. The arms export investigation provided a historic opportunity to settle the double standards of Swedish arms exports. The fact that the government, the Liberals and the Christian Democrats did not take this opportunity and fail to live up to their Congress decisions to stop arms exports to dictatorships is, to say the least, distressing. In May 2011, when the Riksdag decided that the regulations should be tightened up, leading representatives of the Social Democrats, the Environmental Party, the Liberals, the Christian Democrats and the Left Party wrote together at the DN Debate that they committed themselves to a regulatory framework that in practice should not allow the export of war material to dictatorships. It is now clear that the Left Party is alone in standing by that position. Mr President, I would like to A large majority of the Swedish people want to see a ban on arms exports to dictatorships. There is still a chance, together with us, here in Parliament, to change the regulations and to ensure that Sweden stops exporting arms to repressive regimes.

Mr President, I would like to In accordance with the proposal of the investigation, the democratic status of the receiving State shall be a central condition for the examination of permits. Mr President, I would like to Sweden needs a viable defence industry. Ultimately, it is about being able to guarantee a strong Swedish defence capability. In the perspective study presented by the Swedish Armed Forces as late as Friday, it is pointed out that the security situation in the immediate vicinity of Sweden continues to deteriorate. Among other things, the Swedish Armed Forces state four conditions that highlight why Sweden's defence needs to be further developed. First of all, increasing capacity. Russia is planning to continue to increase its military capabilities in the post 2020 period, while our own capabilities are declining with current planning. Secondly, the operational environment in the Baltic Sea region is assessed in the period up to 2035 as regards the purpose, scope and nature of an attack. Thirdly, Sweden will inevitably be affected if an armed conflict occurs in the Baltic Sea region. Fourthly, non-linear warfare is aimed at society as a whole, whose vulnerability to stress increases. Overall, Sweden faces significant security policy challenges in the coming years, and our defence capabilities need to be significantly strengthened. The Swedish Armed Forces also propose an extension of the number of the armed forces and to expand existing capabilities and add new capabilities to the organization. It's about all the combat forces. Being at the cutting edge of technology is not free of charge, and we cannot develop all the materials ourselves. Swedish policy is based on a foundation on which human rights, democracy and the rule of law are fundamental values. Sweden should be an example in its commitment to these values, at home as well as in an international context. We have a long tradition of promoting respect for human rights in key international fora, for solidarity in humanitarian aid and for comprehensive aid. It is therefore important that arms exports do not discourage these efforts. Mr President, I would like to Against this background, the broad agreement on new and strengthened arms export rules presented this summer is welcome. With it we are now taking a big step forward and showing global leadership for democracy and human rights and to prevent war material from being sold to oppressive, non-democratic countries in the future. We are now introducing a democracy criterion that will guide the licensing process as a central condition. If there are major democratic shortcomings in the country that wants to buy Swedish weapons, this should be an obstacle to the granting of export authorisations. The worse the democratic situation in the country, the less room there should be to give such a permit. We are making more stringent demands for respect for human rights. In the past, there have been obstacles to the sale of weapons when there are serious and widespread violations of human rights in a country. This wording is now being changed to a serious and comprehensive one. We are increasing political responsibility. Where there are significant security policy reasons, export authorisations shall be available. Here we in the Green Party wanted to go further and ban arms exports to dictatorships altogether. Nevertheless, an important part of the agreement is that more difficult and controversial cases will be decided politically. Then voters can hold politicians to account instead of the decision being taken by an anonymous authority. We have also tightened the requirements for subsequent deliveries. For larger weapon systems, consequential deliveries are common and spare parts and various updates continue to be sold long after the original purchase. Fewer products will now be counted as successive deliveries, and regular follow-ups will be carried out to ensure that the situation in the country of destination has not deteriorated. Mr President, I would like to The new agreement on strengthened requirements for democracy and human rights is an important step in preventing weapons from being exported to repressive regimes in the future. It is not perfect, and certainly I wish we could have gone further. But we now have a new common framework that represents real steps forward, and I am pleased about that. I therefore support the committee's proposal.

I would like to thank those in this House who have participated in this process, because it was important and valuable. Now we have a bill, and I am glad that we have broad consensus behind it. As has been shown in the debate, it is important for us to be able to get around this, because these are systems and issues that apply over a long period of time. It is a compromise – that is it, and compromises are sometimes hailed as an important basis for creating long-termism. We stick to the unconditional basics. For example, the decisions of the UN Security Council are an unconditional obstacle, and the government cannot take decisions contrary to them. We have tightened up the conditionality barriers that have emerged in the past. It concerns export controls in terms of democratic status, human rights, PGU testing, consequential deliveries and diversion, where the issue of being able to review and follow up with end-user certificates becomes a more important part of the work to ensure that materials are not put into service. We maintain that there are conditional obstacles in the case of countries with an internal armed conflict. One issue that has been very much discussed is transparency and transparency. We would have liked to have seen further clarification there, but we see that there are steps along the way in the compromise proposal. The letter can be clarified, and it is also about information in the Advisory Council on Foreign Affairs. There are steps along the way. We have the question of alternates in the Export Control Council so that the Council function can be more inclusive. There is also a small individual space to be able to handle matters within the framework of confidentiality legislation when everything is decided and debate takes place in the Riksdag. This is something that we will probably have to try and see more of in the future. It also makes clear, which is important, the responsibility of the government. Regardless of which government it is, the government is responsible for export control. It is still a political test to be carried out. It is not a matter to be dealt with in a court of law, but it is a political test to be carried out. In the investigation, we fully agreed that this would be the case. In this squad structure we have an authority whose Director-General has to make decisions, take advice from the Export Control Council if you are unsure about the situation and then hand it over to the government for review. Ultimately, then, it is the government that is always able to lift this up to its own table if you wish, because you are involved in this whole process. This is an important part of getting away from the discussion about who takes responsibility. Is anyone taking responsibility? This was part of what preceded the parliamentary decision in 2011. But here it is clear that it is the government that has the ultimate responsibility. The Government also reports to the Riksdag and to the Advisory Council on Foreign Affairs how it has handled its responsibilities. It is also clear that this is an overall assessment. It is not possible to say that this applies equally to all, but in each unique case the criteria and questions must be examined. It was also a broad consensus on holding the ISP as an authority for this, with the Export Control Council, as an opportunity for practice development as an important element. In the discussion that is taking place, I think there is reason to underline what is really the basis, namely that this is a prohibition law. You must not carry war material listed from Sweden without permission. It is important to emphasise this. But Sweden's security cuts through. If it is in Swedish security policy and defence policy interests, you can break the ban and give the go-ahead. Then, of course, it should be tested on the basis of the criteria that exist and which are also clarified in the bill. So it is not about jobs or anything else that is also included in the debate, but it is ultimately about Sweden's security and Sweden's defence needs. As has also been pointed out, it is not weapons, bullets and gunpowder that are at stake. It is also about different parts, such as war material, other war material and the PDA, i.e. dual-use items. All of this is involved in armaments. This means that Scania trucks from home Södertälje could be included. This means that safety nets can be included. This means that in some cases it may be the need for gunpowder and bullets that may be present. It may be a matter of sheet metal from Borlänge. All of this can be seen as war material, and it must therefore be tested. We had an increase in exports in 2017. However, 82 per cent of exports went to the EU, EEA countries, the USA, Canada, South Korea and Brazil. It was a marginal part that went to other countries. I would like to emphasise that we will see more cross-border cooperation, not least in the EU where the conditions for our joint action will be interesting to test. This applies, in particular, to ancillary delivery issues. But it is a tightening up, and it is important. Mr President, I would like to I note astoundingly that the Swedish Democrats are now calling for the whole proposal to be rejected and that the Left Party is calling for the proposal to be approved, but it has a different opinion on paragraph 2, which deals with guidelines. Mr President, I would like to I support the proposal in the report and reject the reservations.

The work went on for three years, and in June 2015, the investigation presented legislative proposals that were due to enter into force in April 2016. Now we are standing here, almost two years after the deadline, to debate the bill Strengthened export control of armaments. So, did it turn out the way we had in mind? We Christian Democrats are, of course, not satisfied with the fact that the proposal has been long drawn, and in part we would also have liked to see tougher wordings. However, today's decision is important, and we believe that the proposal represents an important strengthening of arms exports. We will vote in favour, and I hereby support the committee's proposal in the report. Sweden needs a strong defence industry. Through participation in international cooperation in the field of defence equipment, Sweden secures long-term security and defence policy interests. The Christian Democrats therefore believe that trade in defence equipment with certain countries is important and also defensible. Exporting and importing war material is part of Swedish defence policy. This presupposes, however, that there are clear rules for who can be the buyer of the equipment we sell. That is absolutely necessary. Otherwise, trade clashes with the values that form the cornerstones of our society. The government often describes Sweden as a humanitarian power – a country that works to save lives. The Swedish government also takes a stand for human rights, freedom and democracy. One cannot claim to stand up for democracy and human rights and, at the same time, export arms to the world's worst dictatorships. Since 2001, the Christian Democrats have taken a clear stand in our programme of principles to introduce a democracy criterion in the export control of armaments. For the Christian Democrats, three issues were in focus during the process of the so-called KEX investigation: the democracy criterion, the question of consequential deliveries and the issue of openness and transparency in particular as regards the work and positions of the Export Control Council, the EKR. The bill contains important statements that the democratic status of the recipient country is to be a central condition when the export of armaments is allowed. The worse the democratic status, the less room should be for permission to be given. In the event of serious and widespread violations of human rights or serious deficiencies in the democratic status of the recipient country, it constitutes an obstacle to authorisation for the export of war material or to other international cooperation related to war material. The authorisation testing shall also take into account whether exports counteract sustainable development in the country of destination. Unless this legislation creates a central obstacle to democracy, changes will have to be made. This is made clear in the Christian Democrats' special opinion on the report. The Christian Democrats believe that there is a need for more transparency and transparency in arms exports. It is good that alternates are now included in the EKR and that the questions are raised to the Advisory Council on Foreign Affairs at regular intervals. This increases transparency, but we would have liked to see the work of the ECR opened up even more so that, after a certain period of time, Members could tell us how they have set themselves to export to certain countries – this in order to give citizens the opportunity to demand responsibility from those who have taken the decisions. i-1024368cfae3c7fa-153	Mr President, I would like to I would first like to express my thanks for a good and enlightening debate this morning. I would also like to express my thanks to the committee for the benevolent treatment of the proposal. However, the great thanks should of course go to the six parties which, after many discussions and considerations in the investigation into the rules on arms exports, managed to agree on the proposals and changes on which the bill is based. The investigation was led by Hans Wallmark in a very meritorious way, as I have heard. I would therefore like to thank you in particular, Mr Hans, for your constructive way of helping to keep this process together in every way very complicated. You have done so in such a way that we can now go to the end of complicated legislation. Everyone knows that this is a difficult issue. There are strong ideological tensions, well-motivated and legitimate positions, strong defence and security interests, strong foreign policy interests and strong industrial interests. On top of that, there is a fact that must also be taken into account, namely that there is a great deal at stake. In the end, these questions are about war and peace, life and death. Sweden is a country which, in any case for the last 200 years, dare I say, has striven for peace and relaxation. Is that compatible with weapons production? Is that consistent with exporting war material? These questions are often raised in the debate. Is Sweden's position and policy in this area compatible with arms production? It depends entirely on your basic attitude. For a pacifist, it is probably two completely incompatible positions to strive for peace and then at the same time to allow the production and sale of weapons, because, at least as I understand it, we want to disarm at all times and thus, by extension, abolish military defence. Then you can't make weapons at the same time. I think that position is honourable, but I do not share it. It is simply unrealistic because it only works if everyone on the planet thinks and acts in much the same way. It has never happened, nor will it ever happen. As soon as there is any country in the world that shows an interest in invading another country, the states in the immediate region will need a military defence, and then they will need weapons. My point of departure is rather that all countries have the right to defend themselves against attacks and indeed also an obligation towards other countries in the region to do so. Otherwise, the field is left completely open to any aggressive attacker. If it has been said that all countries have the right to a defence, it has also been said that they have the right to produce weapons. This also applies, of course, to a country like Sweden. We have a long tradition of armaments production. That, in turn, goes hand in hand with our history of alliance freedom over the past few decades. It is clear that there are strong defence and security reasons for their own weapons production. It's enough to look at the Air Force. If we didn't make JAS, where would we buy airplanes from? Would we buy them from the United States or from Russia? Would we like to commit ourselves to one of these countries? I believe that none of these options is particularly attractive, especially in view of recent developments. If, then, there comes another country, such as Brazil, which wants to buy the JAS from us to defend its borders against an attacker in exactly the same way that we think we need to do and they want to buy from us, on the one hand, because they know it's a damn good aircraft, and on the other hand because they don't want to tie up with the United States or Russia either – is there any sustainable argument for us to say no? It does not, in my view, do so, especially because we also know that we would never be able to finance the development of such an aircraft if we do not also have an export. So, in my view, there is no contradiction in principle between seeking peace and stability and also having an armaments industry. Moreover, if you are a strong supporter of the freedom of alliance, it becomes even more important that there is a Swedish defence industry that gives us, in my opinion, much greater independence. Rather, this argument has been reinforced by the growing international uncertainty of recent years. I heard earlier in the debate that the Swedish Democrats claimed that the Swedish armaments industry is in danger. I think Björn Söder expressed this as a critical situation for the Swedish defence industry. That statement is completely out of the air, I would say. It is enough to look at the export revenue in recent years for the armaments industry in Sweden to see that it is wrong. Last year, revenue was 11 billion. The year before, 2016, they were also around 11 billion, and it was even a small increase of 2 percent. In 2012 it was 10 billion, in 2013 it was 12 billion, then it was a small decrease and then it was again at around 11 billion. There is no drama either in terms of increasing or reducing these revenues, nor is there any evidence to support the claim that this would be a critical situation for the Swedish defence industry. Our position is based on the fact that there is no conflict of principle between these two positions. But it may well be, and it depends entirely on what the export rules are and what kind of business you allow. This contradiction and this question have been taken very seriously in Sweden all these years. On the contrary, we have taken them seriously and have drawn up rules in which every business is examined very carefully. As has been pointed out a number of times during this debate, Sweden has one of the world's most stringent regulations on arms exports. As a matter of principle, weapons exported from Sweden shall not contribute to the deterioration of the international or regional security situation. As a matter of principle, we do not export to countries which, for example, wage war against other countries or which pose international or regional threats. At the same time, it is always complicated that conditions are changing because a country that has previously been peaceful and democratic can suddenly change quite dramatically. Then we must also address this issue, particularly as regards the question of the supply of follow-up supplies. These are, therefore, rather difficult and sometimes delicate balances which the responsible authority ISP has to deal with together with the party-politically-composed Export Control Council. With this bill, one of the world's strictest regulations now becomes even stricter. As the first country in the world, we are facing a particular democracy criterion in the trial. The worse the democratic status of a country, the less room there is to grant export licences. This is an essential tightening up of the regulatory framework, I would say. It will be more difficult to export to non-democratic countries. Is that impossible? No. If there are strong Swedish defence and security policy reasons for implementing a deal, it can be granted, but it requires such a very delicate examination. Nevertheless, if you look at the whole picture, it is a tightening up of the regulatory framework in a way that no other country actually has. Why are we doing this? Well, we know that democracies very rarely wage wars against each other. Lack of democracy in itself is a factor to be put in the balance for security policy reasons. But we also believe that weapons are not just any commodity. Weapons can be used not only to defend one country but also to attack other countries and to maintain internal repression and simply protect the dictators. It is therefore time that we now have an explicit democracy criterion in relation to the test. In addition, we are further strengthening the criterion of human rights. If there are serious and widespread violations of human rights or serious shortcomings in the democratic status of the recipient, this constitutes an obstacle to export authorisations. Once again, it is an overall assessment to be made in which Swedish defence and security policy interests should be taken into account, but it is also a tightening up of the regulatory framework that must be noted. I heard that in the debate the Left Party was trying to spread the image that this does not include women's rights. It's completely wrong. Women's rights are human rights – can we not agree on that in principle? Of course, women's rights will also be a part of the test of this business. Don't spread any other image. What I would also like to highlight now is that with this bill we will have a post-control system. We have not had that systematic way before. Now ISP is tasked with designing such a system. It is simply a matter of ensuring that control is increased, so that we ensure even more that Swedish armaments really end up where it is said to end up and not in any other hands. In order for ISP to be able to fulfil this task and other new tasks, their funding has also been increased by almost 50 per cent from this year. Mr President, I would like to With this, Sweden will have a stronger and tighter arms export legislation. It is a well-balanced proposal. We can represent the decisions ethically and at the same time take responsibility for Swedish defence and security policy and for the long-term conditions of the Swedish defence industry. We do this through a broad cross-block agreement, just as it should be when we deal with these kinds of issues.

The Left Party believes that this is a major flaw. Mr President, I would like to Does responsible arms exports mean that engineers and workshop workers in Sweden become idle? Some people have been into it. Or is it possible to change the war industry so that it works in the service of life rather than in the service of death? Yes, it is. Let me give some interesting historical examples! After the Japanese attack on the American naval base Pearl Harbor in 1941, the U.S. quickly changed the whole of society, especially its industry. In just a few months, huge numbers of fighter planes and weapons of all kinds were built to enter World War II. Now we need to do exactly the opposite and make a massive and rapid transition from military to civil production. The needs are enormous. Here Sweden can go ahead and show the way. But, of course, a clear law is needed that unequivocally prevents the export of war material to countries that should not be armed. We could have a much smaller arms industry. Mr President, I would like to The Lucas Plan is another important example that shows what is possible if only the will exists. Lucas Aerospace was a British company that produced a lot of different products. About half of the production was military. This was in the mid-70s. Suddenly, thousands of staff faced the threat of losing their jobs when orders went down. At that time, a group of employees of the company, both officials and workers, came together and worked out a detailed plan for how the company could produce – as they put it – socially useful products instead of military products. The work took over a year and resulted in over 1,000 pages of proposals. It was about developing new products in renewable energy, for example, but also about increasing the production of things that Lucas already produced, such as dialysis machines. As one of the leaders in this group said: It is not decent and reasonable for people to die waiting for dialysis when we could produce more dialysis machines. This is also relevant here in Sweden, I think. In total, about 150 new products were produced. Many were in the absolute technical forefront. These included, for example, vehicle batteries, wind power, solar cells and medical technology. Had Lucas – and the British government, which was a major client – chosen to listen to the employees, they would have pioneered in a lot of future areas, such as environmental technology, and had good chances of flourishing. Unfortunately, however, it was not done this time, and the company was not doing so well either. The conversion plan nevertheless received a lot of attention, and we have every reason to be inspired by it again. Think of the creativity that could be unleashed if those who work in the Swedish arms industry were instead commissioned to produce socially useful products on a large scale, or for that matter if they took matters into their own hands, like their colleagues at Lucas Aerospace! Perhaps one of them is listening to this debate. Mr President, I would like to Let me mention an interesting calculation example from Lester R. Brown, founder of the Worldwatch Institute and Earth Policy Institute, coined the concept of sustainable development in the 80s. In 2008, he estimated that about 13 percent of the world's military spending would be sufficient to deal forcefully with mankind's fundamental destiny. He lists social objectives such as education for all, maternal and child care in the poorest countries and basic health care for all. He also refers to the restoration of the soil, such as the protection of the soil, the restoration of fish stocks and pastures to the extent possible, and action against climate change, to name but a few. That would be a good security policy. By all means, the figures have probably changed in ten years since Lester R. Brown counted, but the basic principle is the same. The introduction of the LSS and personal assistance is therefore a reform of freedom really worthy of the name, a reform that gave back people their value, a reform that, for those born after the introduction, made it self-evident to be able to live like everyone else. It is a law that guarantees you and me that, regardless of our functionality, our right to live and control our lives should be a matter of course – a foundation that in a democracy should never be questioned. But now it is. Today, the LSS reform is under very great threat, a threat created by political craftsmanship. Imagine that one day your opportunity to go to work, be with your family, or go out with your friends may be priced. One day every toilet visit that I make and every shower and all my meals can take place to the sound of a time clock and with a small note in the corner that I may be a criminal. One day you can be one of the 80 percent who do not get the right to assistance. In the worst of all worlds, the future debate in this House may be about whether your grandchild should have the right to play and have hobbies or just sit and scrap for herself or whether my mother's right to decide over her life should have an age limit. Not one of us is unmoved by what is happening with the assistance. But the threat that now rests upon the LSS is not something that happened by chance. Behind the terrible development we are witnessing at the moment is political action or lack of political action. With shadow curtains and magic tricks, two governments have successively turned our rights into a question of money – a question of what some people in our society are going to cost. But now, with the Social Democrats at the forefront, the reform is seriously threatened. Madam President, I would like We in the Left Party do not protect the LSS. We are fighting for it in solidarity with all those affected today, knowing that reform is for all of us. That is why the amendment proposed by the government here today is not enough for us, because even if we do not put the last nail in the coffin, the exact situation that we have seen escalate during the government's time in power remains. The law must be adjusted to take account of all the judgments that have been handed down. Otherwise, the threat to the LSS remains, and then it is the government that is responsible. We naturally support the committee's proposal, but we also call for the approval of reservation 3.

Madam President, I would like The Government claims that the explanation for the severe cuts is changed case law in the courts and thus in the Social Insurance Office. The precedents have arisen when authorities, i.e. politically governed organisations, have chosen to push a tougher interpretation of the rules to the Supreme Administrative Court. It is not individuals and not the courts themselves who have done so. Secondly, the interpretation of the case law of the Social Insurance Agency has not arisen in a vacuum either. Perhaps, as is often the case in politics, a window was opened so that it was possible for such a fantastic reform to take place for a short time. The LSS legislation had very many clear positive effects. There was no criticism, but the whole thing just rolled on. I was a municipal politician at the time, and I think we were quite proud to show delegations from other countries how well we arranged it for people with disabilities here in our country. But then it started to get smok in the cup. Court trials changed how the law could be implemented by the authorities. In 2009, a judgment narrowed down the concept of a basic need. In 2012, the Supreme Administrative Court concluded that LSS could not be applied if necessary outside the scope of the Social Services Act. In 2015, some efforts were limited to being possible only for those with mental disabilities. These judgments had, of course, a major negative impact on the application of LSS rights. The deterioration became so extensive that the government decided to set up a major investigation in 2016. We saw how the intentions behind the LSS reform were gradually broken out when the law was tried by the judiciary. We saw that we had to defend the rights by trying to rewrite the law so that it would hold to be tried by the courts. As we have heard in previous exchanges in the debate, it has actually been apparent for quite a long time that politics should have taken on this far earlier. Among other things, Karin Svensson Smith and I have long advocated building with concrete. i-1024368cfae3c7fa-515	After 2028, when the Ostlink between Järna and Linköping is completed, you can earn another 10 – 20 minutes on the Stockholm – Malmö route. In an interview in Aftonbladet last week, Göran Persson was asked: Is the high-speed train worth the price? Göran Persson answered accurately: No, I have never understood why we should invest SEK 350 billion to make Copenhagen the centre of the Nordic region. That will be the regional policy consequence. Once a Swedish high-speed railway will be completed, with today's pace of expansion, we will have the world's most expensive and the world's most outmoded railway. Madam President, I would like Ertms, the European Rail Traffic Management System, is a new signalling system for railways. The purpose of Ertms is to enable efficient cross-border train traffic. In Sweden, Ertms has been first used on the Bothnia Line and then on the Ådalsbanan, Haparandabanan and Västerdalsbanan. Next up is the Malmbanan. What did I just say the Ertms is for? Well, Ertms is there to enable efficient cross-border train traffic. How much cross-border train traffic is there up in northern Sweden? Today we have a well-functioning system. The trains are now double equipped. This applies to the Öresund trains, the X2000 trains and also to the so-called EC locomotives operating cross-border trains without any problems whatsoever. The Swedish Transport Administration claims that the expansion of Ertms will cost SEK 30 billion. It is then interesting that Banedanmark claims that the expansion in Denmark will also cost SEK 30 billion. But the difference is that Denmark has only one fifth as much rail as Sweden. Yet Banedanmark claims that it will cost SEK 30 billion. And Trafikverket in Sweden claims that it will cost SEK 30 billion. Who's counting wrong? I would like to say that it is the Swedish Transport Administration and that the Danes have a much better track of what this will cost. I will also say a few words about rail safety. I have mentioned many times in the speaker's chair that we must invest in safety doors on the Swedish trains corresponding to the doors on the aircraft in order to prevent the cutting of trains. Madam President, I would like We must also ban trains with only one employed person on board, i.e. only one train driver. A train wagon smokes in less than a minute, and it is completely lit in less than three minutes. From an indication of fire, it takes at least a minute before the train can stop. After that, safety calls must be made and permits must be obtained before passengers can be released. The delay that occurs with only one, alone, train drivers on board can mean the difference between life and death for travellers. Therefore, the Swedish Democrats believe that a player in today's procured train traffic should not be able to win a tender through rules with reliefs that mean lower safety for passengers. Trains with only one train driver on board should therefore generally be banned, Madam President. I support our reservations Nos 8 and 12.

It has also led to better maintenance of the track itself and of the system used by railway operators. That is how a market economy works, and we are one in our part of the world after all. This applies regardless of whether the customer is someone with a personal name who is going with a passenger transport or whether it is a large or small Swedish company that is going to move goods. In 2013, the then alliance government decided on the initiative of the then Transport Committee to set up an investigation to make a thorough analysis of the Swedish rail system. In December 2015, the investigator, Professor Gunnar Alexandersson, presented his final report. It's called Another Train Order – beyond the Railway knot. The report is not one of the world's most readable documents. It is quite dry, and it covers many sides. But it is also rich in content. The investigator tied many well-known people to his work in the form of experts and users of the Swedish rail system. This is probably one of the largest reviews of Swedish railways ever made. The investigator presents a wide range of proposals aimed at strengthening the rail system and increasing accessibility, reliability and efficiency. I think my conclusion and that of many others is that Gunnar Alexandersson's investigation should be a gold mine for any Minister of Infrastructure and something to put his teeth in. This is, of course, an expert study filled with good proposals on how to make a complicated national rail system work better. But not so – both the then and the present Minister of Infrastructure, Social Democrats Anna Johansson and Tomas Eneroth, chose to make this solid work a shelf heater. It is both spectacular and remarkable that the government has so consistently announced that it will not even allow the investigation to be referred to. For those who listen to this debate, the speeches earlier and the speeches that are coming, it must appear as a mystery that a government chooses to ignore a thorough search that includes so many suggestions for improvements on a social issue that is discussed as much as the Swedish rail system. There is always a great deal of discussion about this. My analysis, which I also think is correct, of why Anna Johansson and Tomas Eneroth have so consistently chosen not to bother with the investigation, is that its proposal and conclusion on continued market opening to achieve efficiency, cost control and increased accessibility in the Swedish rail system did not fall on the lip of the red-green government. The government is ideologically weighted by promises to renationalise the Swedish rail system, and therefore it was quickly assessed that Alexandersson and the other investigators had come to the wrong conclusion. This is probably also the reason why they have not dared to refer to the investigation. The Red-Green Government is afraid to have its policy of renationalising tested in a consultation session in which social actors, rail operators and operators are allowed to give their views on and are likely to confirm many of the findings of the investigation. Those who currently travel either in Västra Götaland or on Tjust and Stångådalsbanar or any of the 26 different regional railways should not see their opportunities cut. On the contrary, we need to rearrange these routes throughout the country. This is a finely branched system, where the small tracks are connected with the big ones. One assumes the other. I also think that the easiest climate action a person can take to facilitate the future of children and grandchildren in terms of reduced climate impact is to travel by train, even if you only do so once. All of us who travel in Skåne can see the difference after the Öresund trains have been expanded. You have not just had to whip people into the trains, but rather there are far too many travelers to make it easy. When I'm home riding in the train system, I admire people who are happy to stand between the stations, although the comfort of the car would of course be much greater. This is probably the biggest climate effort you can make as a private person in Sweden: choosing the train instead of flight or car. Then it is our job as politicians to expand that system, make it more reliable and make sure that things are connected, so that everyone who goes, whether it is local, regional, large-scale or high-speed, has their opportunity to do so in a simple, convenient and fast manner as well as possible. Then there is something between high-speed trains and regional commuting. There are other things that need to be expanded. When we went on our trip to Örebro and Hallsberg we took a great interest in the plans regarding the route between Oslo and Stockholm. There's a lot of points about this. Given how many flights are made between Oslo and Stockholm and how travel times look in Värmland and elsewhere, there is an enormous potential for improvement. The Botanical Corridor has a huge potential to accommodate more people, expand businesses and link this up. It is reasonable that the Botnia Line, which we have spent quite a lot of money on, is better connected with the investment that is made down to Gävle and Uppsala, so that it is possible to travel in 250 all the way and so that eventually you can make the Botanical Corridor a whole link. All of this, I think, is very worth it. But then one must also dare to do as our ancestors did in the 1800s. Sweden's economy and Sweden's public finances were not better then. But courageous decision-makers made the decision to borrow to invest in the future. It is about protecting taxpayers' money by investing in the new. What does this have to do with the future? Yes, I would like to join those who look at transport from a gender perspective. If men were to move as women on average, they would halve the impact of climate change. That's the difference. When building infrastructure that may last 50 to 100 years, such as a road or a railway, one should not plan in view of how men aged 55 move today. They may not be using this railroad or the road for much longer. Instead, plans should be made for those who are young or children today. How do they want to move? How do they do it? What do they think? What are their values? They have a few things in common. Owning a car isn't what defines your personality-- it's one of those things. But I can say that it was to a large extent so especially among the guys of my generation. I was born in the '50s. Not owning a car was kind of weird. Not taking a driver's license was a bit odd. That is not the case for young people today. Transport is much more practical. If you live in the country it is clear that it is pretty good to have a car, but if you live in the city you might rent a car in the summer or are in a car pool. We're gonna plan for that kind of travel. We should not plan for the travelers of the past, but for young people and for an equal transport system. In the Green Party, we take note of this too, but we do not want to make the climate a personal moral issue. Building your own railroad is quite difficult. Asking people to stay at home is also quite difficult. People have to work and make a living and want to get to meetings on time. That is why we must ensure that the railways are extended so that there are good alternatives to aviation and the car. I am very pleased that we have such a good atmosphere in the Transport Committee. Although we have different opinions, we can discuss with each other in an objective way, collect information together and take responsibility for what is our task as legislator and decision-maker in the Swedish Parliament. I support the proposal in the report.

i-1024368cfae3c7fa-714	Madam President, I would like Days like these with harsh weather and cold provide a good framework for today's energy debate. The energy issue is not only about finding the most environmentally smart solutions, it is also about ensuring that the lights always shine, that computers can always be started and that machines in industry can always continue to produce. It is important for Sweden's competitiveness and business and people's everyday lives. Sweden may be a small country to the population, but Sweden is an extremely large country to the surface. Most of the electricity generation is in northern Sweden, while most of the electricity consumption is in southern Sweden. This difference will become even more apparent with further closures of nuclear reactors in southern Sweden. It also leads to a higher proportion of volatile electricity production and a decreasing share of base power, which places high demands on Svenska Kraftnät's ability to maintain the power balance in the grid. For this reason, the Swedish Parliament decided in June on an announcement in which the Swedish power grid is given an even more extensive task to analyse the power situation. That announcement has been pushed by Moderates, and we hope that the government will continue to take the announcement seriously and follow it up. On several occasions, the Moderates have also highlighted the need to shorten the processing times for network investments. Sweden's competitiveness is threatened by new economies that are growing rapidly. At the same time, it takes up to ten years for the network expansion required for a new industrial company to establish itself here. In ten years China has doubled the size of its economy. So the whole gross domestic product has been doubled. It's the same time it takes to expand a network investment. Every extension should of course take place in a legally secure manner, carefully analysed from a societal perspective and in consultation with relevant stakeholders. But ten years is actually an eternity today. Although the government has set aside funds to shorten processing times, requirements are also needed to ensure that carrots are effective. We therefore want to see sharper measures to shorten the processing times for network investments. It is also in line with another announcement that the Riksdag has approved, i.e. that authorities that are in contact with companies should also have time limits for their processing times. In several chamber debates on energy, I have pointed out that there is a need for us in Sweden to become more global in our energy policy and more technology positive. The Moderates therefore have several claims aimed at raising their eyes and strengthening Sweden's ability to export clean electricity and energy technology to other countries. But almost half of the SEK 45 billion that will need to be invested in the Swedish core networks over the next ten years is due to needs based on system reinforcements and market integration. This tempts Swedish power grid's financial ratios properly, and indebtedness is escalating faster and faster. In order to finance the greatly increased investment rate, it is expected that the charge for the power charge will have to be doubled over the same period. In its system development plan, Swedish power networks have made a brief comment about their financial position in relation to the investment needs: The financial ratios for a private company would indicate an unhealthy financial position, and it would be difficult and costly for the work to finance the investments unless Swedish power networks were part of the state and can borrow from the National Debt Office. The irony of the whole is the genuine lack of financial incentives in several stages. The energy policy pursued creates a need for network reinforcements due to the subsidy-driven expansion of production capacity – in order to export production at a price well below the actual cost of production to other countries. This is something that is not sustainable in the long term. Madam President, I would like The power balance in Sweden has been strong for a long time. It has only been due to the large-scale expansion of hydroelectric power and later also to the investment in nuclear power. This has contributed to the fact that Sweden, as one of few nations, is not dependent on fossil-based power generation. Despite this, several parties in this House are raising the need for an energy transition for Sweden, as is being discussed at European and global level. But Sweden's power system does not need an energy shift. We already have a power system characterised by a high capacity factor, zero greenhouse gas emissions and a high level of security of supply. It is understandable, however, whether there is an energy shift on the agenda at global and European level because the power supply there is exclusively fossil-based. But an energy transition in Sweden will instead mean that we generally get a lower capacity factor, increased greenhouse gas emissions from the Swedish power system and reduced security of supply. For the Swedish Democrats, it is incredible with what power and speed one tries to ruin something that has served Sweden and our industry so well for so long. Madam President, I would like As you know, Sweden now has a negative power balance for what you call a ten-year winter. This is supposed to be a scenario for the future. For me, it feels more like a look back to the conditions one had in Sweden during the 19th century, when one had to hold back and rely on the forces of the weather in order to develop the economy. i-1024368cfae3c7fa-754	Madam President, I would like The Swedish electricity network is built up by three different levels: local grids, regional grids and core networks. If one were to extend the Swedish electricity grid in a single long line, it would last more than 14 laps around the world. The core network is owned by the state and managed by the Swedish power network. Instead, the regional and local networks are managed through so-called concessions, i.e. the state has given an operator the opportunity to operate, maintain and manage the network. The regional networks are mainly owned by three companies: Eon, Vattenfall and Ellevio. There are about 160 local electricity grid companies in Sweden, but how large areas they handle varies greatly. Some small companies manage only a few kilometers of management, while others own thousands of miles. It is now 20 years since the electricity market in Sweden was liberalised. The aim was to increase competition in electricity supply. It would lead to lower prices, it was said then. However, we can see that liberalisation has, on the contrary, led to significantly higher charges. Private electricity grid companies are currently allowed to set very high charges. Fees also vary widely across the country. In addition, the process is characterised by frequent appeals by companies in order to increase profits. Networking is a so-called natural monopoly and, with the liberalised market and inadequate regulation, private companies are given great leeway. Electricity network charges charged by electricity grid companies are largely determined by where you live, and it can distinguish thousands of crowns. Despite the energy agreement between the government, the Moderates, the Center Party and the Christian Democrats, no response to the rising electricity grid charges has been presented. In a situation where both ordinary households and Swedish industry can be hit hard by further increases, you choose to let the market handle the matter. To date, this has only led to higher electricity prices for both households and industry. Greater political responsibility is needed to counter the excessive price increases for consumers. The legislation governing the revenue framework is currently too arbitrary. The Electricity Act should therefore be amended to counter unfair price increases for consumers. It is unreasonable that there should be such large differences in network charges between cities and countries in Sweden, between regions and between different companies. It can vary as much as SEK 7,370 per year between the same households depending on whether you live in Grums in Värmland or Ljungby in Småland. Raising electricity grid charges not only hit households hard, but also hit industry and, by extension, also the economy and our jobs. There is also a risk that the necessary switch to a safer and more reliable electricity network will not take place at the necessary pace, since the various companies have different economic conditions, i.e. that they do too much in profit. In order to ensure the availability of electricity, an electricity network is required that is adapted to the needs that exist. It is clear that profit-maximizing companies have no interest in keeping prices down or taking into account the social impact of increased charges. To this end, more uniform electricity grid tariffs should be introduced to offset the inequalities currently affecting rural areas. The extension of main grids for electricity in Sweden is greatly lagging behind, and large parts of the existing grid need to be renewed. In Svenska Kraftnät's perspective plan 2025, the need for investments and reinvestments is estimated to amount to SEK 55-60 billion until 2025. By way of comparison, the investments made between 2000 and 2011 amounted to only SEK 10.6 billion. Today there is a major problem with bottlenecks in the transmission of electricity to consumers through the electricity grids. A lack of transmission capacity between price areas 2 and 3 means that we cannot make full use of the energy from our rivers or the storage capacity provided by hydropower. This means that the surplus from the northern part of the country cannot benefit the southern parts sufficiently. As new wind turbines connect to the grid, today's problems worsen. Particularly worrying is that today's electricity grid is not enough for the expansion of wind power in northern Sweden to benefit the whole country. It is often easier to obtain permits for the expansion of wind turbines in the northern part of the country. In order to increase electricity generation in southern Sweden, both the abolition of the municipal veto and the expansion of offshore wind power are important. A change in the electricity certification system that also takes into account the balance of power could also be a step in the right direction. However, it is of the utmost importance that the network capacity be expanded so that the electricity can be transferred to southern Sweden, where it is consumed. It should therefore be assigned to Swedish power networks to prioritise the planned reinforcements of the core network. In order to secure electricity supply throughout the country, renewable energy needs to be expanded to a greater extent also in southern Sweden. There, offshore wind power will play a major role. The expansion of wind power on Gotland is also an opportunity that should not be underestimated. However, a key to enabling such an extension is a well-functioning transmission capacity. The work on the planned power cable between Gotland and the mainland has been interrupted by the Swedish power grid. It is a mistake that risks hindering important deployment of renewable energy. In addition, the security of electricity for the whole of Gotland is at risk, as has not least been proven by the summer's extensive power outage. Swedish power networks' actions are short-term and a step in the wrong direction. The government should therefore instruct the Swedish power grid in its regulation letter to build the planned power cable to Gotland. In this context, the importance of a Nordic consensus on cross-border transmission capacity should also be mentioned. The review of Svenska Kraftnät's economic targets under way within the Government Office is expected to form the basis for new return targets. In a situation where Swedish power networks face major investment needs, it is not reasonable to pay hundreds of millions each year to the state. The owner directives for the Swedish power network should be clear in that the public benefit should be before profit distribution. The state process for core network conc essences needs to be speeded up. Today, the authorisation process for the core network takes an unreasonable amount of time. The bottlenecks in the electricity grid and the slow licensing process are two of the problems the Swedish National Audit Office highlights in its report. The reasons for the problems identified by the Swedish National Audit Office include the previous government's inaction in the governance of the Swedish power grid, its lack of feedback to the Riksdag about the reasons for the unfulfilled investment plans of the Swedish power grid and the lack of common guidelines for the county boards for their efforts in the licensing process for core networks. The government should therefore instruct the responsible authorities to investigate the possibilities of a faster licensing process for core network concessions. On this point, today we have a common reservation with the bourgeois parties. Madam President, I would like Electricity production needs to be seen as part of society's infrastructure, which we jointly build on societal goals. For the Left Party, it is essential that the electricity grid is owned by society through, for example, the state or the municipality. The technological transformation of the energy system with small-scale energy production means that the energy consumers of the future will at the same time become the energy producers of the future. Therefore, the network that links them together needs to be owned by the public and not limited by private profit interests. Liberalisation has not led either to lower prices or to greater security for consumers. It's just the opposite. It is clear that the Swedish electricity market needs to be fundamentally redesigned. There is reason to believe that the Swedish power network's decision not to proceed with the plans for a third Gotland cable may need to be reviewed. Correct and efficient condition processes are an important building block in the creation of our future energy system. It is therefore positive that the Swedish Energy Market Authority has taken many measures to streamline licensing processes with a focus on shorter processing times. One measure is the introduction of a new process-oriented approach where there are always several administrators who are jointly responsible for a number of cases according to a structured process. Of course, the fact that more officers are working on one and the same matter means stability and that it does nothing if a case officer becomes ill, because the work can still continue. The Energy Market Inspectorate has also employed more staff. Another measure is to provide companies with better information so that they send in complete application documents from the outset. The Government allocated increased funds in the budget for the Energy Market Inspectorate to streamline the concession process. So that work continues and will continue. As I said, it is unusually cold in Sweden this week. Wind power continues to account for a relatively large part of electricity generation, and we also owe our regulatory power to hydroelectric power. Yesterday, Sweden exported 725 megawatts of electricity to our neighbouring countries according to Swedish grid statistics. A winter day like this increases electricity consumption in Sweden by about 200 megawatt hours for every degree as it gets colder. This connection is because we use electricity for heating. So far, Sweden has chosen to work with a power reserve of power plants that can be started up at short notice to meet the need for extra power cold days. Reducing the need is another possibility. To dimension the electrical system after a few days with really cold winter should be weighed against cost-effective measures to reduce power peaks or control the load. Some electricity consumption can be controlled to other hours or days with relatively simple technology. Storage technology is also interesting in this context. With today's technology, much of this can be managed automatically without the customer experiencing any change. On behalf of the Government, the Swedish Energy Market Authority has produced a report on demand response that presents around 20 proposals for a smarter energy system, where customers' situation is strengthened and they have a better chance of influencing their electricity costs. These proposals are now being prepared at the Government Office, and I look forward to the Parliament being given the opportunity to decide to implement them. An important part of this is also the constant work on energy efficiency. We need to be better at doing more with less energy, and there is great technological potential. Energy efficiency is something everyone benefits from – society, the private economy and the environment.

These are global needs and therefore Sweden also has a responsibility. In January 2016, the Government presented its new industrialisation strategy, Smart Industry. Within this framework, Growth Analysis has, among other things, been able to identify the need for minerals and metals for environmental and technological innovations. Growth analysis reported the assignment at the end of 2017. In the report, Growth Analysis describes the future need for innovation critical metals and minerals. The work was followed up at the end of 2017 and the government gave SGU an assignment that had two parts. One concerns the mapping of these innovation-critical metals, especially those that are important in the environmental and climate transition. The second part is at least as important, and there you look at the availability of raw materials from secondary sources, that is, how we should make use of what is in the mining waste itself. This is something we generally need to get better at. How can we recycle and reuse to a greater extent? In a world of increasing population, we will need to be better at closing the loops. We want a corresponding ban on oil and gas extraction in Sweden. The government has shown its willingness to do this but has not yet been put to the fore. This means that we need to put an end to the decline in the mining industry and, at the same time, become better at creating cycles for what is broken. i-1024368cfae3c7fa-1124	Madam President, I would like As an advocate of nationalising school activities, I have to say that it is extremely strange to talk about nationalising school food as the most important issue today. Fortunately, there has been quite a lot of consensus in this Parliament about preschool.
