In addition, it belongs to the institutions of Swedish society that enjoy the greatest confidence among the population. Most important, however, is that the children who attend preschool appreciate it. Good childcare is as important for children's development as a good school, if not more important. Therefore, the right to good childcare for all children should also be self-evident. Good childcare is also a prerequisite for women and men to be able to work on equal terms. That is why it is also a feminist effort. We want an educational activity with as high quality as possible. We therefore opposed the introduction of a childcare allowance. Childcare allowance means that all municipalities are forced to introduce a system without taking any account of local conditions. Unfortunately, there are major shortcomings in educational childcare. In a report from 2016, the Swedish School Inspectorate notes that the number of individual authorities in the field of educational childcare has increased, while the Swedish National Agency for Education concluded in 2012 that a market has emerged in the field of educational care, where individual authorities compete with each other and with municipalities as principals. Companies sometimes also take advantage of the fact that it is today the right of establishment for pre-school activities. At the same time, the School Inspectorate warns that children in the organisation may risk being under-stimulated if the person in charge lacks educational competence or if there are no children of the same age in the group. In small groups of many different ages, some children are at risk of being squeezed when the business cannot be adapted to everyone. The Swedish School Inspectorate also warns that they have visited activities where day care providers have had a lack of knowledge of the Swedish language, which has made it difficult for children to communicate. The Left Party sees the importance of giving all children the opportunity to attend preschool in order to develop to their full potential. At the same time, we realise that it is not possible to abolish educational care in all places. For example, in rural and rural areas, it may be particularly difficult to solve childcare with the current options. There may also be a need for consideration of older staff who have worked with this throughout their working life. However, we believe that the issue of the long-term abolition of educational childcare needs to be investigated. A prerequisite for a really good school system is that it focuses on what is most important. Quality and needs should always come first, not the opportunity to enrich themselves in the business. In school and preschool there should be staff so that each child receives the support they need, something that must not be prioritised down to create profit distribution to the owners. To get there, the school needs to be commercialized, and so does preschool. The Left Party believes that the goal of the school should be to give all children the best learning, knowledge and care, not to generate profit to private companies. Sweden is today one of the most extreme countries in this particular area. That is why we are pleased to have put forward, together with the government, a proposal on welfare gains. This has now finally come to light as a result of the profit investigation that Ilmar Reepalu has produced. I would also like to apologise here and now for having to leave in advance to talk about this issue with Ilmar Reepalu at a lecture here in Stockholm. Previous efforts are something we in the Left Party think it is important to invest in. We want to continue developing preschool. Therefore, it is also important to help and support children in need of special support already in preschool. Early intervention will make it easier for these children in preschool, school and the rest of their lives. According to the pre-school curriculum, activities must provide incentives and special support for children who are in difficulties of various kinds. Today, there is very little follow-up on whether the curriculum is actually being followed, as well as how it works in the transition from preschool to preschool class.

i-1024368cfae3c7fa-1294	Mr President, I would like to Today's debate is about preschool, as we have all understood now. It is that part of the education system that is aimed at the youngest children, those who have not yet started primary school. Today, preschools have their own national curricula and goals. The goal of preschool is to offer safe care and to stimulate children's development and their desire to learn. Preschool is the place of play pedagogy. Here the children get the opportunity to fantasize, pretend, practice empathy and get to know each other's experiences in the game. It is in the game that the child develops his social ability and prepares for what is to come. It all started in Germany with a man named Friedrich Fröbel. In 1837 he started a preschool, or a kindergarten. He said that the game is the heart of the coming life, for in the game is revealed man's deepest disposition, his innermost being. It's nice. In Sweden came the kindergarten in the late 1800s. Kindergarten became a children's house, which became a day care center. The 1968 children's study submitted its proposal, and preschool became the accepted concept for the operation. In connection with this, responsibility was also transferred from the Ministry of Social Affairs to the Ministry of Education. It was a clear indication of what the business task was. In doing so, the educational content was given a much more prominent role and a curriculum of its own. In preschool there have also been development of activities with different educational strategies such as Reggio Emilia, Waldorf, Montessori and clocks and showers, which parents can be given the opportunity to choose where the activities are located. Preschool has a curriculum since 1998. It was revised in 2016. They'll stick to it. That's who's running the business. Preschool became its own school form in 2004. Based on the curriculum, principals and staff will form a well-functioning preschool for all children. The municipalities have supervision of the activities, but there are both municipal and private principals. For most children in our country, preschool is the start of their journey in lifelong learning. Preschools are of great importance for children's development and provide children with a level playing field for the start of school. We know that research shows that children who have attended preschool are more successful in school than those who have not. This applies in particular to children who have parents with a short education or children who, for various reasons, live in vulnerable situations. Preschool is an important start in the Swedish school system. We can see that children who have been admitted to preschool perform better even in grade 9. Therefore, it is important that preschool staff have good conditions for the educational work with the children. The government invests in smaller groups of children, and it is beginning to have an impact on the activities. This gives the educational staff better opportunities to meet each child. Play and the pleasurable learning are the hallmarks of children's development, and curiosity should be captured and reflected throughout the business. A high quality should characterize the entire preschool. The children should feel equally seen no matter who they are. It is important that staff have a good education in counteracting traditional gender patterns and roles and that each child has the opportunity to develop at its own pace and on the sides that for them fall naturally. Ensuring good and nutritious food for children also plays a crucial role in the well-being of children. It is about using food as an educational instrument for the knowledge of how we function as biological beings while highlighting the issue of how food is produced. Happily, 44 percent of the food served in preschool is organic. Meals are also occasions when children receive social training. Preschools are, of course, facing major challenges. We all know that. Equality and equality are two concepts that are incredibly important, not least when we talk about preschool. Making children an equal future citizen is an important task for the educators who work there. The Government is actively working with efforts to support positive development. One of these is that preschool is part of the initiative Collaboration for best school. It is an initiative aimed at pre-schools in socio-economically weak areas. The Government allocates SEK 100 million to preschools in these areas, among other things, to develop and invest in multilingualism. Mr President, I would like to The year in which the child turns six leaves preschool and moves into the preschool class. The transfer between preschool and preschool classes has been clarified in a revision of the curriculum, as well as the importance of accurate information about the content of the education and who is responsible for transmitting information. Another important step is taken in a young person's life. I support the committee's proposal in the report and reject the motions.

i-1024368cfae3c7fa-1331	Mr President, I would like to According to the report Statistics on public procurement, presented last autumn by the Swedish Procurement Authority and the Swedish Competition Authority, the value of the procurement obligations in Sweden corresponds to approximately SEK 642 billion. These are enormous sums, equivalent to more than a sixth of Sweden's gross domestic product, which affects a large part of Swedish business, regardless of the size of the companies. Public procurement is therefore of strategic importance for jobs and growth. The aim of public procurement is basically good. Contracting authorities shall make the best use of public funds by taking advantage of competition on the market while competing on equal terms. In the words of the Procurement Authority, an efficient purchasing process provides a basis for creating good business. We all benefit from that. However, one might ask whether the good purpose is achieved. In more than half of the tenders, between zero and three tenders are received. It is a real problem, and it should be analysed in greater depth why, and if so, there is something we legislators can do to change it. Sweden needs to be a country where all companies – big and small, with or without collective agreements, who can and want – have the opportunity to participate in public procurements to sell good quality goods and services both within the country and on export. Through effective competition, quality can increase while keeping prices down. This is urgent, not least for welfare services. Quality combined with economy and our common resources should be one of the top priorities of the public sector. As a small, trade-dependent country, new export opportunities mean increased tax revenues as well as more companies and jobs. In order for this to become a reality, we need to create good conditions for our Swedish companies so that they become able to bid and also win them internationally. In this way, Swedish companies can reach more and wider markets in Europe. Such a strategy takes its start in Sweden. Today, however, many companies show that it is difficult to participate in procurement. The procurement documentation is often impenetrable and must be processed and answered with short response times. For example, by requiring experience of having delivered a number of similar deals or being able to deliver large deals, the possibility is limited for many or when the public imposes unreasonable demands, which drive up the price and can also collide with each other. As an example of the latter can be mentioned Fruit Delivery Sweden, which supplies fruit baskets for subscription to private companies and public customers. They have several public customers who require class 1 labeling of fruit, which no single company does. Class 1 is about the most expensive fruit, but it does not have freshness or taste to do without appearance. The fruit must not be oblique or differ in any millimetre in size. We can politically push the issue of reducing food waste. But the public sector sometimes goes in the opposite direction, knowingly or unconsciously, and I do not know which is the worst. At worst, the public sector contributes to a deterioration of competition in the market, resulting in oligopoly or monopolistic situations. It risks making processes, services and products more expensive for citizens. We also miss knowledge in innovative companies with new business ideas. There are several good examples of how innovation procurement succeeds in other countries, for example in Norway. By cooperating with and taking advantage of knowledge and expertise in the business community, the chance to find new, good and creative solutions for different societal problems increases. This is an important development, not least in terms of welfare, where we face major challenges in terms of population development. In June 2016, the Government put a bill on Parliament's table on a new regulatory framework for public sector procurement based on three EU directives on public procurement from 2014. Parliament rejected parts of the bill, because it went beyond what was required by the introduction of the EU directives. The Swedish Parliament called on the government to come back with a new proposal, so that the necessary EU directives could be implemented in Sweden. This proposal came last spring and is better, but far from good. The proposal that was adopted goes beyond what the European directives require and, in our view, means difficult to interpret and unclear legislation. Our concerns were and are that there is a great risk that small businesses will be disadvantaged. Many smaller companies feel that it is too complicated to tender for public contracts. They're participating anyway. In 2016, more than three quarters of the bidders were micro or small enterprises, but they accounted for only half of the contracts signed. It is important that the requirements of procurement law and in practice exclude smaller companies. The requirements shall be permeated by clear, reasonable and equivalent conditions. Our most central criticism concerns when specific labour law conditions are to be imposed. Our second objection concerns the cases in which a main contractor should impose requirements also on subcontractors throughout the supply chain. The latter poses a risk of complications for small businesses, even though the new rules only apply above the threshold. The consequences for small companies acting as subcontractors can be unreasonable. We Moderates want to safeguard the purpose of public procurement. We want the public sector to spend tax money on the right things, to keep costs down and to reduce waste. In this way, we safeguard the legitimacy of our tax system and thus the basic prerequisite for financing what the public sector delivers, regardless of whether it is the state, county councils or municipalities that procure. There is a risk when making additional demands – whether collective agreements, enhanced animal welfare, environmental requirements or requirements on the appearance of fruit – and trying to solve social problems other than those that public procurement is the tool to solve. On the other hand, the public sector should make greater use of the opportunities offered by functional and innovation procurement. These can help solve our social problems in ways we may not know about. The government has noted the lack of statistics in the procurement field, and the issue is being addressed. It is important that we have a good basis for assessing how this strategic area works so that we see where we, as legislators, may have to screw the conditions. I support the committee's proposal under paragraph 2 and reservation 8.

The animals are killed and bled without stunning, which means a prolonged death course with unnecessary suffering, pain and anxiety. The ritual slaughter method I'm talking about is what's called halal or koscher slaughter. Undeaf animals — sheep, lamb, and cows — are trapped in a position that can be described as a tumble drier, turned upside down in an unnatural and painful way to finally have their throats cut off. Nerves, arteries, food and trachea are cut off with the knife, but it does not go deep enough, which means that the blood continues to pump up to the brain. After this, the animal can live up to eleven minutes before it finally meets its fate and dies of blood loss. A veterinarian describes the procedure in the following way: It is a huge operation without anaesthetic. In all other circumstances, as a veterinarian, I would immediately be deprived of my identification if I did something similar. In Sweden, slaughter without stunning is still prohibited, but according to the former CEO of the Meat and Chark companies, it is common for many Swedes without knowing if it gets slippery slaughtered meat or kosher meat on their dinner table. Slaughterhouses in other countries over-produce ritually slaughtered meat in order not to lack it, and are then exported and sold as normal meat in other countries. In a 2011 report, the French Ministry of Agriculture estimated that 40 to 60% of the animals were slaughtered without stunning, despite the fact that the demand for halal and kosher meat accounted for only about 10% of the slaughter. According to a study by Svenska Dagbladet, none of the country's largest charkgrossists could account for either control paper or contract texts that guarantee that meat from animals that have been tormented to death does not reach the Swedish consumers. As there is a market for these products, and therefore also financial incentives, more and more meat producers are moving towards those slaughter methods. A report from the European Commission shows that up to 75% of cattle in some EU countries are slaughtered without stunning. In some other producer countries all sheep and lamb meat is slaughtered in this way. It is difficult to say exactly how best to tackle this growing problem, but the Swedish Democrats believe that the problem needs to be tackled from several angles, which the motion also addresses. On the one hand, Sweden must take a strong stance towards the EU in favour of general bans on this brutal form of slaughter throughout the Union, and, on the other hand, Sweden must actively seek derogations in the EU so that we can ban imports of meat slaughtered using methods that deviate from the intentions of Swedish animal welfare legislation. Meat from animals slaughtered and bled without stunning must also be labelled with a clear warning to enable consumers to opt out of these products before Sweden has developed an import ban. What is relevant to the Finance Committee is the request to require that all food procured in public procurement is guaranteed to be from animals slaughtered in accordance with Swedish animal welfare rules. Sweden may have the most ambitious animal welfare in the world, Mr President, and that is something that we Swedish democrats are proud of and want to protect. We believe that a compromise with Swedish legislation and, above all, ethical standards should never be accepted. I support the Swedish Democrats' reservation 6. Finally, I would like to say a few words about the Alliance-wide motion 2017/ 18:3412, which the SD supports in this report. The exercise addresses an important aspect of public procurement given the complex regulatory framework that companies face in the procurement process. For small businesses that lack the time and resources needed to understand in detail the procurement requirements, there will of course be a high threshold to cross. This represents a loss both for entrepreneurship and for taxpayers. It is therefore urgent that we should now be able to address an announcement to the government on this issue. SD will always be on the side of small businesses. We saw this not least last year when the procurement bill was being dealt with, which had excluded a majority of Swedish companies from public procurement if it had been passed. The main reason why we stopped the original proposal was that the proposal would distort competition between posted workers on the one hand and Swedish workers on the other. Contractors who imposed conditions that went beyond the so-called hard core would only apply to Swedish companies while foreign companies were excluded from the rules, which would lead to a competitive advantage for companies with posted workers. Of course, we could never accept this, and that is why we pressured the government to revise the proposal so that there would be a level playing field and fair competition in the Swedish labour market. The new revised proposal was subsequently voted through with our support.

Mr President, I would like to The LOU, the Public Procurement Act, contains fundamental principles of equal treatment, transparency and non-discrimination that protect the supplier, and it also allows authorities to benefit from competition in the market and ensure that citizens' paid taxes are as beneficial as possible. Most seem to agree that public procurement rules can and should be simplified. Many suppliers experience procurement as bureaucratic processes, and a large majority therefore choose not to tender. I myself am an entrepreneur and know how time-consuming it is to count on detailed tenders for volumes that are not known to be met. It is therefore gratifying that the committee has supported the alliance motion, which says that public procurement requirements should be permeated by simple, clear and equal conditions regardless of the size and form of the company. Making it easier for small businesses to participate in procurements means that small family farms without collective agreements can participate in the municipal procurement of food. Then the children of my municipality of Mora may receive locally produced food on their plates from the Morell family's farm. Mr President, I would like to On 30 November 2016, the Riksdag voted through large parts of the Government's proposal for a new regulatory framework for procurement. When the Public Procurement Act was introduced in 1994, the Law Council, which reviews all legislation, said that the law was too comprehensive and detailed. The Law then contained 132 paragraphs. The current Procurement Act contains about 1,000 paragraphs. The council's criticism this time needed 418 pages to fit in. The criticism was, to put it very mildly, brutal. Among other things, the Swedish Law Council said that the law has acquired a level of scope and detail that is generally not desirable and that may be considered contrary to the normally applied legislative technique in Sweden. During the period from 18 April 2016, when the EU directives were supposed to have been implemented in Swedish legislation, until 1 January 2017, when the new procurement laws came into force, there was considerable uncertainty among both suppliers and contracting authorities as to what rules were actually in force. I'm sure you remember that, all of you who were there at the time. For example, the Procurement Authority said that LOU and LUF, which are both procurement laws, would be used but interpreted on the basis of EU directives. Therefore, the Center Party has urged the government to evaluate the impact of the new procurement legislation and evaluate the impact on public procurement that began between 18 April 2016 and 1 January 2017, when we had a very unclear legal position. Unfortunately, the Committee on Finance proposes that this very reasonable request be rejected by Parliament. Of course, I am in favour of reservation 1 in the report, which deals with this very subject. Mr President, I would like to Finally, I would like to draw attention to two motions written by my centre party colleagues Per-Per-Ingvar Johnsson, Eskil Erlandsson and Rickard Nordin. They point to the absurdity of the fact that we here in Parliament are making demands on Swedish food production and on Swedish farmers, but that these requirements when the public then procures food are not included in the procurement documentation. What is the effect of this? Yes, we place high demands on Swedish farmers who increase their costs, but in the procurement the Swedish farmer loses because the demands we have made on him have reduced his competitiveness. In the end it may be that we do not have any Swedish farmers left because of the Swedish special requirements. I say as farmer Martin Moraeus in Orsa: What is allowed to eat in Sweden should also be allowed to produce in Sweden.

i-1024368cfae3c7fa-1382	Mr President, I would like to We are here to debate the FiU34 report by the Committee on Finance, which deals with some forty motions from the general period of exercise relating to public procurement. The public sector in Sweden, through, for example, government agencies in the state, municipalities and county councils, as well as municipal and state-owned companies, deals each year for more than SEK 600 billion. This is mainly regulated by procurement legislation, which aims to maintain competition in the market and to use tax resources in a cost-effective manner. The selection of suppliers shall always take place on a commercial basis and shall be based on the quality of the goods or services in question and the conditions under which they can be delivered. In order to ensure a cost-effective service of as high quality as possible, it is therefore important that we affirm innovation and that we acknowledge that more actors and suppliers want to participate in public procurement. In its budget proposal, the government states that the lack of competition in public procurement can be very damaging to society and citizens and lead to lower quality citizens and taxpayers and less innovation at a high price. I agree. However, one of the many challenges we face is that SMEs today feel that it is complicated – iblan d too complicated – to participate in public procurement at all and therefore choose to abstain. Studies show that only 27% of SMEs participate in public procurement. It is therefore positive that the committee has supported the alliance exercise 3412 on public procurement requirements. The claim and the statement there is that public procurement should be permeated by simple, clear and equal conditions regardless of the size and form of the company. Mr President, I would like to I would then like to dwell on two of the Liberals' proposals in the report. In order to develop and encourage more SMEs to participate in procurement, the new Procurement Authority or the Competition Authority should be given the task of setting up a government platform for public procurement. Inspiration is available from many other EU countries, for example from the Danish State Portal udbud.dk. A government platform would allow many more smaller-sized suppliers to actively participate in procurement procedures, thereby strengthening competition and improving quality overall. In addition, transparency would increase for citizens as they get a better overall picture of public sector purchases – what is actually bought, by whom it is purchased and at what cost. Mr President, I would like to Citizens want to be able to choose from a variety of practitioners, whether they are home care, childcare, health care or care for the elderly. You simply want to be able to choose. Competition and diversity also promote innovation and dynamism. It is therefore important for laws and regulations to benefit and not hinder all kinds of serious companies in the welfare sector. A neglected category is the non-profit or idea-based enterprises, which are founded and run by social entrepreneurs. The share of idea-based companies is currently 3%, and the figure has been static over the last ten years. At the same time, the development of us differs from our neighbours Denmark and Norway, with similar welfare systems, with a share of 14% and 7% respectively. The Liberals, in their second reservation, have tried to make this visible and want to see opportunities to acknowledge the opportunities offered by ideas to companies to participate in public procurement.
